❯ Take a look at this codebase, telll me what it does                           

  Read 7 files, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)
  ⎿  Loaded lib/CLAUDE.md

⏺ LitGraph is a web app for exploring academic literature. You start from one
  paper, look through what it cites and what cites it, collect the relevant
  papers, and use LLMs to write a review or check a manuscript's citations.

  What it does

  1. Citation-graph explorer (the core feature)
  - You enter a paper ID: a Semantic Scholar ID, DOI:, ARXIV:, PMID: or NBER:.
    NBER IDs are converted to something Semantic Scholar recognizes on the
    server (lib/papers/paperIdResolver.js).
  - It loads that paper's citations and references from the Semantic Scholar
    Graph API, ranks them, and draws the top ~15–18 on each side as an
    interactive D3 force graph (frontend/src/graph/).
  - Clicking a node shows its abstract and details, loaded only when needed.
    "Expand Node" adds that paper's own neighbors to the graph. You can lay the
    graph out by year or let it arrange itself, and choose between "Top
    relevance" and "Lesser-known sample" when expanding.

  2. Review cart and synthesis
  - You add papers to a cart. It can generate a structured literature-review
    draft with Anthropic's API (lib/review/), or export the cart as BibTeX or a
    list of identifiers.

  3. Manuscript citation audit (lib/audit/, frontend/src/audit/)
  - You upload a manuscript (PDF or DOCX, read with pdfjs and mammoth). An LLM
    pulls out every sentence that cites something, plus the bibliography.
  - For each claim, it finds the cited sources, often from your Zotero library,
    picks the most relevant passages from them, and judges whether those
    passages actually support the claim.
  - There is an optional second check using TypeSafe, which can be turned off,
    run in "shadow" (evaluate-only) mode, or turned on.

  4. Citation import
  - It extracts the bibliography from a PDF using GROBID, matches the entries to
    known papers, and imports them into Zotero automatically. It can also read
    RIS files.

  5. Auth
  - Users sign in with Zotero OAuth, and only accounts on an allowlist can use
    the app. An optional token protects the audit feature.

  Stack

  - Frontend: vanilla JS ES modules and D3, with no framework or build step.
  - Backend: shared code in lib/, run two ways: an Express server for local dev
    (server/src/index.js) and Vercel serverless functions for deployment (api/).
    Both use the same handlers.
  - Upstream services: Semantic Scholar (behind the app's own proxy, which
    handles caching, retries and rate limits), Anthropic, Zotero (the local
    desktop app or OAuth), GROBID and TypeSafe.
  - Tests: npm test, using node --test over tests/frontend and tests/server.

  Other things in the repo

  - experiments/: test runs of TypeSafe for evidence checking (with a written
    report) and of citation-import pipelines.
  - CITATION_WORKFLOW_SPEC.md: a design proposal, not yet built, for "justified
    citations": recording where and why each source is cited, with collaborators
    able to review those reasons.
  - ARCHITECTURE.md: notes on a recent refactor that grouped the code by feature
    and removed duplicated server code.

  The README only fully covers the graph explorer and review features, so it's
  partly out of date. The audit, Zotero and GROBID features came later and are
  described mainly in .env.example, ARCHITECTURE.md and the code.

✻ Cooked for 26s · done 3:18 PM

❯ SO an idea i had is the following... If a user wants to extract citations on  
  demand, they could pay to start the grobid service.                           

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 2 files (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Before getting into the idea, here's how GROBID is wired today. The app calls
  an external GROBID wrapper (GROBID_API_URL + EXTRACTION_API_KEY). It makes one
  synchronous POST to /extract?mode=references with a 120s timeout
  (lib/citation-import/grobid.js). The server that runs GROBID isn't in this
  repo, so I'm assuming it's an always-on box you pay for.

  The idea is workable, but I'd split it into two separate decisions.

  1. Scale-to-zero: start GROBID only when needed

  This is where the savings are, and you can do it without payments.
  - Cold start is the main cost. GROBID is a JVM service that loads its models
    at startup and needs roughly 4GB+ RAM. A cold start usually takes 30–90s,
    and longer with the deep-learning models. With a 120s timeout and Vercel
    function limits, a single blocking request won't hold up reliably.
  - So the flow has to become async: extract starts or wakes the service and
    returns a job ID, then the frontend polls a status action (status already
    exists) while showing "warming up…", and runs the extraction once the
    service is ready.
  - Keep it warm briefly after use, maybe 10–15 minutes, so a user importing
    several PDFs only waits through one cold start.
  - Hosting: Cloud Run (min instances = 0), Fly Machines (auto-stop/auto-start)
    or Modal can do this for you. You'd mostly just get a slower first request,
    not orchestration code to write.

  2. Charging users for it

  - Charge for extractions, not for starting the server. Users don't think in
    servers. Credits like "1 credit = 1 PDF" or "a 10-pack for $X" are easier to
    understand, and the cold start stays an internal detail.
  - The numbers don't suit paying per click. A CPU-only GROBID run costs well
    under a cent per PDF on scale-to-zero hosting, while Stripe takes about
    $0.30 + 2.9% per payment. That points to prepaid credits or a subscription,
    not a payment each time.
  - It's a big architectural change. The README says there's no server-side
    database, but payments need stored balances, a Stripe webhook endpoint,
    protection against double-processing the same payment, and refunds when an
    extraction fails. Your Zotero sign-in already gives each user a stable ID,
    so that part is covered.
  - There's already a cheaper fallback. The audit flow pulls bibliographies out
    with Anthropic (lib/audit/auditService.js, extract). You could make that the
    free path and sell GROBID as the paid "precise" option, or the other way
    round.

  Questions that decide the design

  1. Where does GROBID run now, and what does it cost per month?
  2. Is the goal to cut your hosting costs, or to make money? If it's cost, part
     1 alone probably gets you most of the way.
  3. Who would pay: just the allowlisted users, or are you opening the app to
     the public?

  Once you've decided, I can sketch the async job flow or the credits system in
  more detail.

✻ Worked for 25s · done 3:20 PM

❯ so there are two akash setups i have (one on gpu and one on CPU), i just      
  don't want to be continuously hosting it.                                     

⏺ Akash doesn't scale to zero, so getting "on demand" means your app creates and
  closes Akash deployments itself.

  What starting it on demand involves on Akash

  Each start is a new deployment, not a wake-up:
  1. Create the deployment from your SDL, which puts a deposit into escrow.
  2. Wait for provider bids, about a minute, and accept one to create a lease.
  3. Send the manifest. The provider pulls the GROBID image (several GB) and
     starts it. GROBID then loads its models.
  4. Read the lease status to get the new URI. It changes every time, so
     GROBID_API_URL can't stay a fixed env var.
  5. Poll GROBID's health endpoint (/api/isalive) until it's up, then run the
     extraction.
  6. Close the deployment after it's been idle for a while to stop paying.

  Expect about 2–5 minutes from click to ready, sometimes more if no provider
  bids or the image pull is slow. That's too long for a spinner, so the UX
  should be a queued job: "Extraction started, we'll have results in a few
  minutes".

  What the app needs for this

  - A place to store state, like Vercel KV or Upstash Redis, holding the current
    deployment ID, its URI and status (starting / ready / closing), the
    last-used time, and a job queue. Serverless functions can't keep it in
    memory.
  - Locking, so two users clicking at once don't launch two deployments.
  - An idle reaper. A Vercel cron every few minutes would close the deployment
    once it's been idle about 15 minutes. This is what keeps the cost down, so
    it has to be reliable, ideally backed by a maximum lease lifetime.
  - Akash credentials on the server. Either a wallet key in env, which is risky
    because it can spend funds, or the Akash Console API with an API key and a
    managed/credit-card wallet. I believe the Console API supports this, but I'd
    want to check its current docs before building on it.

  Use the CPU setup for this

  The app only calls mode=references, which runs fine on CPU. A GPU mainly
  speeds up GROBID's deep-learning models for full-text parsing. GPU leases also
  cost more and get fewer bids, so they're slower to start. On-demand should
  use the CPU setup.

  A simpler option to compare against

  GROBID on CPU on Cloud Run with min instances = 0, or Fly Machines with
  auto-stop, gives you real scale-to-zero. A cold start is about 30–90s, you
  don't write any orchestration, and at your volume it probably costs a few
  dollars a month or less. Building the Akash path is several days of work plus
  the job flow. If you'd rather stay on Akash, that's fine, but it's worth
  knowing what each choice costs you.

  Where payment fits

  Once the job flow exists, adding payment is a gate before step 1: a user
  spends a credit to start a session, and the warm period covers any other PDFs
  they extract.

  Do you want me to look into the Akash Console API to see whether a server can
  create and close deployments with just an API key? That decides whether the
  Akash route is practical.

✻ Crunched for 20s · done 3:21 PM

❯ i could do cloud run, and the cost is more standard, right?                   

⏺ Yes. Cloud Run's pricing is standard pay-per-use: you're billed for
  vCPU-seconds and memory-seconds while the container is starting or handling
  requests, and nothing while it's scaled to zero. There's no escrow and no
  lease bidding, and the bill comes through normal GCP billing.

  Rough cost for GROBID

  These are from memory, so check the current pricing page.
  - Rates: about $0.000024 per vCPU-second and $0.0000025 per GiB-second.
  - Free tier each month: about 180k vCPU-seconds and 360k GiB-seconds.
  - Example: with 2 vCPU and 4 GiB, a session with a cold start plus an
    extraction takes about 90 seconds. That's 180 vCPU-s and 360 GiB-s, roughly
    half a cent.
  - Free tier coverage: about 1,000 sessions a month. After a request the
    instance usually stays warm for a few minutes without charging you, so a
    follow-up PDF is fast and free.
  - Other costs: storing the image in Artifact Registry is a few cents a month.

  Things to set up

  - Image: use the CPU-only GROBID image (the lighter CRF-only variant if you
    can). It's smaller and starts faster. You don't need a GPU for
    mode=references.
  - Settings: min instances 0, max instances 1–2 to cap spending, concurrency
    fairly low (GROBID uses a lot of memory), and startup CPU boost turned on to
    shorten the cold start.
  - Auth: your wrapper's X-API-Key check keeps working. Otherwise you could use
    Cloud Run's own IAM auth.
  - App changes are small: point GROBID_API_URL at the Cloud Run URL. The
    current 120s timeout should cover a 30–90s cold start plus extraction. Check
    that your Vercel function's maximum duration is longer than that. A
    friendly "warming up, this can take a minute" message in the UI would help.
  - Budget alert: set one in GCP so a bug or abuse can't run up a bill without
    you noticing.

  This changes the payment question

  At about half a cent per extraction, charging users probably costs you more in
  Stripe fees and work than it brings in. Since access is already limited to
  allowlisted Zotero accounts, a per-user rate limit (say N extractions a day)
  plus the max-instances cap likely covers the risk. Payment is worth adding
  only if you open the app to the public.

  Do you want me to write the Cloud Run deploy setup (Dockerfile or gcloud run 
  deploy command, with those settings) and add the warming-up message to the
  frontend?

✻ Worked for 17s · done 3:24 PM

❯ is it possible to start the server once when they click and subsequent clicks 
  are cheaper                                                                   

⏺ Yes. Cloud Run does that by default, and you can make the first click feel
  faster too.

  How it works

  - First click: Cloud Run starts an instance. You pay for the cold start
    (30–90s) plus the extraction.
  - Later clicks: the instance usually stays alive for up to about 15 minutes
    after the last request. With request-based billing (the default; don't turn
    on "CPU always allocated"), you aren't charged for that idle time. Later
    extractions start straight away and you pay only for the 10–20s of
    processing.
  - After a quiet period, the instance shuts down and the next click is a cold
    start again.

  The one catch is that Cloud Run usually keeps the idle instance around but
  doesn't promise to. Sometimes a later click will hit a cold start anyway.

  Starting it before they click

  You can begin the cold start as soon as the user shows they're about to
  extract, for example when they open the citation-import panel or choose a PDF:
  1. The frontend calls a new POST /api/import/warm action.
  2. The backend sends a cheap request to GROBID's /api/isalive health check and
     doesn't wait for the full startup.
  3. Cloud Run starts booting while the user is still choosing a file. By the
     time they click Extract, it's often ready.

  A warm-up that's never used costs only the startup seconds, a fraction of a
  cent. Route it through your backend so it's covered by the allowlist check,
  and limit it to about one per user per few minutes.

  If you need a guarantee

  You could have the app set min instances to 1 on the first click and back to 0
  after 15 minutes of inactivity. That keeps the instance alive for certain,
  but you pay for the idle time, and you're back to running a shutdown job like
  the Akash plan needed. I'd start with the default behaviour plus the warm-up
  and only add this if users keep hitting cold starts.

  Do you want me to add the warm action (to
  lib/citation-import/citationImportHandler.js and the frontend import flow)
  along with the Cloud Run deploy setup?

✻ Brewed for 11s · done 3:25 PM

❯ right so when the zotero login happens, you can warm up the cloud run, right? 
  that makes sense in the workflow                                              

⏺ Yes, that fits the workflow, and it covers the most common path: log in, then
  import. A few details make it work well:

  - Warm when the app loads with a valid session, not only on the OAuth 
    callback. Users with a saved session cookie skip the login step, so warming
    only in the callback would miss them. Running it after the frontend confirms
    the user is signed in covers both cases.
  - Also warm when the import panel opens. If someone logs in, explores the
    graph for 20 minutes and then imports, the instance from login will have
    shut down. A second warm when the panel opens fixes that, and it does
    nothing if the instance is already up.
  - The cost is small. Users who log in but never import still cause a cold
    start, about $0.003 each, and most of that falls inside the free tier.
    Rate-limit warming per user so reloading the page doesn't trigger repeated
    starts.
  - Do it from the frontend, not a server background task. Vercel can freeze a
    function as soon as it sends its response, so a fire-and-forget ping from
    the callback may never go out. The frontend can call the warm endpoint
    without waiting for it, and the endpoint can wait briefly on the health
    check.

  The flow:

  app load (session valid) ──► POST /api/import/warm  ──► GROBID /api/isalive 
  (starts Cloud Run)
  open import panel        ──► POST /api/import/warm  (does nothing if already 
  warm)
  click Extract            ──► /api/import/extract    (usually warm by now)

  Do you want me to build this: the warm action with its per-user rate limit,
  the two frontend triggers, and the Cloud Run deploy config?

  ⎿  1 file changed (binary, mode only or too large to show)
     deploy/cloud-run/deploy-grobid.sh

  Ran 2 shell commands · 1 failed (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The GROBID warm-up is built, and the test suite passes (136 tests). I haven't
  committed it or deployed anything to Cloud Run, and I haven't tried it against
  a real cold start.

  When it warms up

  1. App load with a signed-in session: it checks /api/zotero/status and warms
     if you're signed in.
  2. Right after a Zotero sign-in, from any tab: it listens for the existing
     litgraph-zotero popup message.
  3. Opening the Import panel: covers the case where the instance has gone idle
     since login.
  4. Extracting a PDF: the status now says the extraction service "can take up
     to a minute to start".

  Signed-out users never trigger a warm-up, and there's a test checking that.

  What I changed

  - lib/citation-import/grobid.js: new warm() that sends a GET to GROBID_API_URL
    + GROBID_WARM_PATH (default /) with the API key and waits up to 8s. Any
    non-5xx response counts as ready; a timeout or 5xx means it's still
    starting. It never throws.
  - warm action exposed as POST /api/import/warm, which works on both Express
    and Vercel. It sits behind the existing allowlist and same-origin checks.
  - frontend/src/citation-import/grobidWarmup.js (new): fires the warm-up
    without waiting. The browser sends at most one every 5 minutes, and a failed
    or signed-out attempt doesn't block the next one.
  - deploy/cloud-run/deploy-grobid.sh (new): deploys to Cloud Run with min
    instances 0, max 2, 2 vCPU / 4 GiB, request-based billing and startup CPU
    boost. The API key comes from Secret Manager, and it prints the
    GROBID_API_URL to set on Vercel.
  - .env.example: documents GROBID_WARM_PATH and GROBID_WARM_WAIT_MS.

  One change from the plan

  I limited warm-ups globally rather than per user. There's only one GROBID
  service, so once any user has warmed it, there's no reason for another user to
  ping it again. The server skips pings for 60s after a ready response, and
  repeated reloads are capped by the browser's 5-minute limit. On Vercel the
  server-side limit only holds within one function instance, so in practice the
  browser limit does most of the work.

  Before you deploy

  The wrapper isn't in this repo, so the script guesses three things about it.
  Override them with env vars if they're wrong:
  - Port: GROBID_PORT, default 8070.
  - Key variable: GROBID_KEY_ENV, the env var the wrapper reads its key from;
    default EXTRACTION_API_KEY.
  - Warm path: set GROBID_WARM_PATH to a route that only answers once GROBID has
    finished loading. If the wrapper answers / before GROBID is ready, a
    warm-up can report "ready" too early. Extraction still works in that case;
    the first one just takes longer.

  Also use your CPU image for Cloud Run. The GPU build isn't needed for
  reference extraction.

  Do you want me to commit this on a branch?

※ recap: You want GROBID on scale-to-zero Cloud Run so you're not paying for always-on hosting, and the sign-in warm-up and deploy script are built with all tests passing. Next is your call on committing it to a branch, which hasn't happened yet. (turn off recaps in /config)

❯ Do i need all these env variables in a cloud run                              

⏺ No. Most of those variables aren't for Cloud Run. There are three separate
  places, and Cloud Run needs almost nothing.

  1. The Cloud Run service (GROBID wrapper): one variable
  - EXTRACTION_API_KEY, or whatever name your wrapper reads its key from. The
    deploy script puts it there from Secret Manager.
  - Cloud Run sets PORT itself, so you don't add it.
  - Nothing else from .env.example goes here. Anthropic, Zotero, Semantic
    Scholar and the rest belong to the LitGraph app.

  2. The LitGraph app on Vercel: two required, two optional
  - GROBID_API_URL (required): the Cloud Run URL the script prints.
  - EXTRACTION_API_KEY (required): the same key as on Cloud Run.
  - GROBID_WARM_PATH (optional): defaults to /. Set it only if your wrapper has
    a route that answers once GROBID is ready.
  - GROBID_WARM_WAIT_MS (optional): defaults to 8000. You can leave it unset.

  3. The deploy script: settings on your machine only
  GROBID_IMAGE, GROBID_KEY_SECRET, GROBID_PORT and the rest are only read by the
  script when you run it. They never become env vars anywhere. Only two are
  required:

  GROBID_IMAGE=<your CPU wrapper image> GROBID_KEY_SECRET=extraction-api-key
  deploy/cloud-run/deploy-grobid.sh

  The others already have defaults. GROBID_PORT must match the port your wrapper
  listens on: 8070 by default, or 8080 if the wrapper uses Cloud Run's PORT.
  GROBID_KEY_ENV must match the variable name the wrapper reads its key from.

  So in practice you set one secret on Cloud Run and two variables on Vercel.

          cat > app.py <<'APP_SOURCE'                                           
                                                                                
          import os                                                             
                                                                                
          import secrets                                                        
                                                                                
          import hmac                                                           
                                                                                
          import logging                                                        
                                                                                
          from contextlib import asynccontextmanager                            
                                                                                
          from typing import Literal                                            
                                                                                
                                                                                
          import httpx                                                          
                                                                                
          from fastapi import FastAPI, File, HTTPException, UploadFile          
                                                                                
          from fastapi.responses import Response, JSONResponse                  
                                                                                
                                                                                
          GROBID_URL = os.getenv("GROBID_URL",                                  
          "http://127.0.0.1:8070").rstrip("/")                                  
                                                                                
          MAX_BYTES = 50 * 1024 * 1024                                          
                                                                                
          API_KEY = secrets.token_urlsafe(32)                                   
                                                                                
                                                                                
                                                                                
          @asynccontextmanager                                                  
                                                                                
          async def lifespan(app: FastAPI):                                     
                                                                                
  logging.getLogger("uvicorn.error").warning("EXTRACTION_API_KEY=%s", API_KEY)  
              async with httpx.AsyncClient(timeout=httpx.Timeout(50,            
  connect=5)) as client:                                                        
                  app.state.client = client                                     
                  yield                                                         
                                                                                
                                                                                
          app = FastAPI(title="Local GROBID API", lifespan=lifespan)            
                                                                                
                                                                                
                                                                                
          @app.middleware("http")                                               
                                                                                
          async def authenticate(request, call_next):                           
              if request.url.path == "/extract" and not hmac.compare_digest(    
                  request.headers.get("x-api-key", ""), API_KEY                 
              ):                                                                
                  return JSONResponse(status_code=401, content={"detail":       
  "Invalid X-API-Key"})                                                         
              return await call_next(request)                                   
                                                                                
                                                                                
          @app.get("/health")                                                   
                                                                                
          async def health():                                                   
              try:                                                              
                  result = await                                                
  app.state.client.get(f"{GROBID_URL}/api/isalive", timeout=5)                  
              except httpx.RequestError:                                        
                  raise HTTPException(503, "GROBID is unavailable")             
              if result.status_code != 200 or result.text.strip().lower() !=    
  "true":                                                                       
                  raise HTTPException(503, "GROBID is not ready")               
              return {"status": "ok", "grobid": "ready"}                        
                                                                                
                                                                                
          @app.post("/extract", response_class=Response,                        
                    responses={200: {"content": {"application/xml": {}}}})      
          async def extract(file: UploadFile = File(...),                       
                            mode: Literal["fulltext", "header", "references"] = 
  "fulltext",                                                                   
                            segment_sentences: bool = False):                   
              try:                                                              
                  content = await file.read(MAX_BYTES + 1)                      
              finally:                                                          
                  await file.close()                                            
              if len(content) > MAX_BYTES:                                      
                  raise HTTPException(413, "PDF exceeds the 50 MiB limit")      
              if not content.startswith(b"%PDF-"):                              
                  raise HTTPException(415, "Upload a PDF file")                 
              endpoint = {"fulltext": "processFulltextDocument", "header":      
  "processHeaderDocument",                                                      
                          "references": "processReferences"}[mode]              
              data = {"consolidateHeader": "0", "consolidateCitations": "0",    
                      "consolidateFunders": "0", "includeRawCitations": "1"}    
              if mode == "fulltext":                                            
                  data["segmentSentences"] = str(int(segment_sentences))        
              try:                                                              
                  result = await app.state.client.post(                         
                      f"{GROBID_URL}/api/{endpoint}", data=data,                
                      files={"input": (file.filename or "document.pdf",         
  content, "application/pdf")},                                                 
                      headers={"Accept": "application/xml"})                    
              except httpx.TimeoutException:                                    
                  raise HTTPException(504, "GROBID processing timed out")       
              except httpx.RequestError:                                        
                  raise HTTPException(503, "GROBID is unavailable")             
              if result.status_code == 204:                                     
                  return Response(status_code=204)                              
              if result.status_code != 200:                                     
                  status = 503 if result.status_code == 503 else 502            
                  raise HTTPException(status, f"GROBID returned HTTP            
  {result.status_code}")                                                        
              return Response(content=result.content,                           
  media_type="application/xml")                                                 
          APP_SOURCE                                                            
                                                                                
          exec python -m uvicorn app:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8000

       1 # GROBID on Cloud Run
       2 
       3 Scale-to-zero PDF bibliography extraction for LitGraph. One Clou
         d Run service runs two containers:
       4 
       5 | Container | Image | Port | Resources | Exposure |

  Status: ✅

  Why 1 CPU instead of 0.5: Cloud Run's docs say CPU below 1 requires the
  first-generation environment and concurrency 1. With concurrency 1, a warm-up
  ping and an extraction arriving at the same time would start a second
  instance. The extra half CPU costs a little only while a request is running.

  What I couldn't check: The YAML parses and fills in correctly, but I had no
  GCP project to deploy it to. The risky parts are the container-dependencies
  annotation and the sidecar startup probe. If gcloud run services replace
  rejects either, the error will say which.

  Your remaining steps:
  1. Run the one-time GCP setup in deploy/cloud-run/README.md.
  2. Run GCP_PROJECT=<id> deploy/cloud-run/deploy-grobid.sh.
  3. Set GROBID_API_URL (printed by the script) and EXTRACTION_API_KEY (from the
     secret) in Vercel and in .env.

⏺ The wrapper itself is working; the problem is that no GROBID container is 
  running.

  - Your wrapper container (grobid-wrapper:test) is up on port 8000, on the
    grobid-test network, with GROBID_URL=http://grobid:8070. That's all correct.
  - But it's the only container on grobid-test, and there's no GROBID container
    at all, not even a stopped one. So grobid doesn't resolve to anything, and
    the wrapper returns GROBID is unavailable.
  - curl localhost:8070 returned nothing because nothing on your machine is
    listening on 8070.

  Either the GROBID container was never started, or it was started with --rm,
  exited, and was removed. Start it on the same network under the name grobid:

  docker run -d --name grobid --network grobid-test -p 8070:8070
  grobid/grobid:0.9.1-crf

  -p 8070:8070 is only there so your curl localhost:8070/api/isalive works; the
  wrapper doesn't need it. GROBID takes about 30–60s to load its models, so poll
  until it's ready:

  until curl -s localhost:8070/api/isalive | grep -q true; do sleep 3; done;
  curl -i localhost:8000/health

  /health should then return {"status":"ok","grobid":"ready"}. If the GROBID
  container exits instead, check docker logs grobid. Leave out --rm while
  debugging so the logs survive.

  You could also run KEEP_RUNNING=1 deploy/cloud-run/local-test.sh. It starts
  both containers with docker compose, runs the checks, and leaves them up on
  127.0.0.1:8000. GROBID isn't published to the host there, so check it through
  the wrapper's /health. That script passed earlier on this machine.

⏺ Your local setup is working now. I restarted the wrapper container with docker
  start wrapper, and /health returns 200 {"status":"ok","grobid":"ready"}.

  What went wrong:
  1. First: the GROBID container didn't exist, so the wrapper couldn't reach it
     and returned 503.
  2. Second: once GROBID was running, the wrapper container had been stopped (a
     clean exit, probably Ctrl+C or docker stop), so nothing was listening on
     port 8000.

  Both containers are now running in the background on grobid-test. To try an
  extraction against them, use the key the wrapper container was started with:

  curl -s -H "X-API-Key: $EXTRACTION_API_KEY" -F
  "file=@paper.pdf;type=application/pdf" \
    "localhost:8000/extract?mode=references" | head -c 500

  To point the LitGraph app at this local setup, set
  GROBID_API_URL=http://localhost:8000 and the same EXTRACTION_API_KEY in .env.

⏺ I've updated the deploy script: if you set WRAPPER_IMAGE, it skips Cloud Build
  and deploys that image. That's committed on grobid-cloud-run. Run the
  following from the repo root, on that branch.

  1. Build and push the wrapper
  PROJECT=grobid-extraction-avi
  IMAGE=us-central1-docker.pkg.dev/$PROJECT/grobid/wrapper:$(git rev-parse
  --short HEAD)
  docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64 -t "$IMAGE" --push
  deploy/cloud-run/wrapper

  2. Create the key secret and let Cloud Run read it
  openssl rand -hex 32 | tr -d '\n' | gcloud secrets create extraction-api-key
  --data-file=- --project $PROJECT

  PROJECT_NUMBER=$(gcloud projects describe $PROJECT
  --format='value(projectNumber)')
  gcloud secrets add-iam-policy-binding extraction-api-key --project $PROJECT \
    --member="serviceAccount:$PROJECT_NUMBER-compute@developer.gserviceaccount.c
  om" \
    --role=roles/secretmanager.secretAccessor

  3. Deploy
  GCP_PROJECT=$PROJECT WRAPPER_IMAGE="$IMAGE" deploy/cloud-run/deploy-grobid.sh
  This deploys service.yaml, with GROBID as a sidecar next to your wrapper. It
  then opens the service to public requests (the wrapper still checks the key
  itself) and prints GROBID_API_URL.

  4. Test it
  URL=$(gcloud run services describe grobid --region us-central1 --project
  $PROJECT --format='value(status.url)')
  KEY=$(gcloud secrets versions access latest --secret=extraction-api-key
  --project $PROJECT)
  time curl -s "$URL/health"     # first call includes the cold start, about 
  30-90s
  curl -s -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' -F
  "file=@paper.pdf;type=application/pdf" "$URL/extract?mode=references"   # 
  expect 401
  curl -s -H "X-API-Key: $KEY" -F "file=@paper.pdf;type=application/pdf"
  "$URL/extract?mode=references" | head -c 300

  5. Point the app at it
  Set these in Vercel (Production and Preview) and in your .env:
  GROBID_API_URL=<$URL>
  EXTRACTION_API_KEY=<$KEY>
  GROBID_WARM_PATH already defaults to /health, so you don't need to set it.
  Redeploy on Vercel so it picks up the new values.

  Things that could go wrong:
  - Step 3 is the first time the service YAML meets real GCP. If gcloud run 
    services replace rejects it, the likeliest cause is the
    container-dependencies annotation or a sidecar probe. Paste me the error and
    I'll fix it.
  - "Domain restricted sharing" error on the allUsers binding: your organization
    blocks public services, and we'll need a different approach.
  - Set a budget alert under Billing → Budgets & alerts as a safety net.

✻ Worked for 17s · done 6:08 PM

❯ do i need to push changes to github to get vercel to do a redeployment        

⏺ No. Changing environment variables only needs a redeploy, and you can do that
  without pushing anything:

  - Dashboard: Deployments → open the current Production deployment → ⋯ →
    Redeploy. It rebuilds the same code with the new environment variables.
  - CLI: vercel redeploy <production-deployment-url> does the same thing.

  Production will still be running the old code, though, because the warm-up
  feature is only on the unpushed grobid-cloud-run branch. That's fine for now.
  The old code calls the same /extract endpoint with the same key, so PDF
  imports will go to Cloud Run. Users just won't get the warm-up, so the first
  PDF after an idle period waits through the cold start (30–90s, inside the
  existing 120s timeout).

  To get the warm-up live:
  - Usual route: push the branch and merge it into main. If the Vercel project
    is connected to GitHub, that deploys automatically.
  - Avoid: vercel --prod straight from your branch. It works, but production
    would then run code that isn't on main, and the next push to main would
    quietly replace it.

  So: redeploy now to switch to Cloud Run, then merge the branch whenever you're
  ready. I can push it and open the PR if you want.

✻ Baked for 25s · done 6:11 PM

❯ "The provider is busy. Wait before retrying." where is this error?            

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ That message comes from lib/citation-import/citationImport.js:69:

  if (response.status === 429) throw new ProxyHttpError({ code:
  'citation_rate_limit', message: 'The provider is busy. Wait before retrying.',
  status: 429, retryAfterSeconds: Number(response.headers.get('Retry-After'))
  || 60 });

  It isn't from GROBID. It's in the shared helper the import service uses for
  its calls to Crossref (looking up references) and Zotero (creating the
  collection and saving items). It fires when either of them returns HTTP 429,
  "too many requests". A GROBID failure would say "GROBID extraction failed (…)"
  instead.

  To tell which provider it was, look at the status message just before the
  error:
  - "Matching references…": it was Crossref. This is the more likely one,
    because the app sends one Crossref request per reference, back to back.
  - "Saving to Zotero…": it was Zotero. Zotero's own "slow down" header produces
    a different message ("Zotero requested a pause"), so this would be a plain
    429 from Zotero.

  Why it happens more than it needs to:
  1. No contact email in the Crossref requests. The requests identify themselves
     as LitGraph/0.1 (citation import pilot). Crossref gives better rate limits
     to requests that include a mailto: contact (their "polite pool"). Without
     one, LitGraph lands in the public pool.
  2. Shared IP addresses on Vercel. Vercel's outbound IPs are shared with other
     apps, so Crossref can count their traffic against you.
  3. No automatic retry. The server sends back how long to wait
     (retryAfterSeconds, 60s by default), but the frontend's lookup loop stops
     at the first error and leaves the retry to you.

  Possible fixes:
  - Add a server-only CROSSREF_MAILTO env var and include it in the Crossref
    requests. The address would come from your config, not hard-coded.
  - Have the frontend wait for retryAfterSeconds and retry automatically, a
    limited number of times, showing "Crossref is rate-limiting; retrying in N
    s…".
  - Add a short delay between lookups.

  Which step were you on when it appeared? If it was "Matching references…", I'd
  do the first two fixes.

⏺ Bash(vercel logs --help 2>&1 | head -5)
  ⎿  (eval):1: command not found: vercel
  ⎿  (another command ran in this repository at the same time; a change 
     made by either may show under either result)

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ It's Crossref. Clicking "Import to Zotero" extracts the references and then
  immediately starts looking each one up on Crossref. The lookups are what hit
  the rate limit.

  I ruled out the other services: an Anthropic 429 would say "Model request
  failed (429)…", and a GROBID problem would say "GROBID extraction failed…".
  The only code that produces "The provider is busy" is the Crossref/Zotero
  helper. Saving to Zotero only starts after every lookup has finished, so
  Crossref is the only fit.

  I sent Crossref one request using the app's exact User-Agent, and its response
  headers show how strict the limits are now:

  x-api-pool: public-array
  x-rate-limit-limit: 1
  x-rate-limit-interval: 1s
  x-concurrency-limit: 1

  That's 1 request per second, 1 at a time, for anonymous callers. The app sends
  its lookups back to back, one per reference, so with a fast response the
  second lookup arrives within the same second and gets a 429. On Vercel it's
  worse, because the outbound IPs are shared with other apps hitting Crossref.

  The fix has three parts:
  1. Add a contact email for Crossref. A new server-only CROSSREF_MAILTO setting
     would add mailto: to the User-Agent. That moves the requests into
     Crossref's "polite" pool, which has more generous limits.
  2. Space the lookups out, at least one second apart, so a normal import stays
     under the limit.
  3. Retry automatically on a 429: wait the Retry-After time and try again a few
     times, with a "Crossref is busy; retrying in N s…" status, instead of
     stopping the import.

  Do you want me to build this? You'd add CROSSREF_MAILTO to Vercel with
  whatever contact address you want Crossref to see.

      141 +// Lookups are paced to the limits Crossref reports (public po
          +ol: 1 search/s, one at a time;                                
      142 +// polite pool: 3/s, three at a time) and retried with backoff
          + when the provider answers 429.                               
      143 +const pace = { concurrency: 1, spacingMs: 1100, nextAt: 0 };  
      144 +const MAX_LOOKUP_RETRIES = 3;                                 
      145 +const sleep = ms => new Promise(resolve => setTimeout(resolve,
          + ms));                                                        
      146 +async function nextSlot() {                                   
      147 +  const now = Date.now(), at = Math.max(now, pace.nextAt);    
      148 +  pace.nextAt = at + pace.spacingMs;                          
      149 +  if (at > now) await sleep(at - now);                        
      150 +}                                                             
      151 +function learnPace(limit) {                                   
      152 +  const rate = Number(limit?.limit), intervalMs = Number(limit
          +?.intervalMs), concurrency = Number(limit?.concurrency);      
      153 +  if (rate > 0 && intervalMs > 0) pace.spacingMs = Math.max(10
          +0, Math.ceil(intervalMs / rate * 1.1));                       
      154 +  if (concurrency > 0) pace.concurrency = Math.min(Math.floor(
          +concurrency), 4);                                             
      155 +}                                                             
      156 +async function lookupRow(row) {                               
      157 +  for (let attempt = 0; ; attempt++) {                        
      158 +    await nextSlot();                                         
      159 +    try { return await request('lookup', { doi: row.doi, bibli
          +ography: row.bibliography, title: row.title }); }             
      160 +    catch (error) {                                           
      161 +      if (error.status !== 429 || attempt >= MAX_LOOKUP_RETRIE
          +S || stop) throw error;                                       
      162 +      const waitMs = Math.min(Math.max(Number(error.retryAfter
          +Seconds) || 2, 1), 60) * 1000 * (attempt + 1);                
      163 +      pace.concurrency = 1; pace.spacingMs = Math.min(pace.spa
          +cingMs * 2, 5000);                                            
      164 +      pace.nextAt = Math.max(pace.nextAt, Date.now() + waitMs)
          +;                                                             
      165 +      message(`The lookup service is busy; retrying in ${Math.
          +ceil(waitMs / 1000)} s…`);                                    
      166 +    }                                                         
      167 +  }                                                           
      168 +}

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've fixed both problems: arXiv references now resolve through DataCite, and
  Crossref lookups run faster and recover from rate limits. The work is
  committed on a new branch, import-arxiv-crossref (commit 289f55b), but not
  pushed. All 144 tests pass, and I checked the new lookups against the real
  Crossref and DataCite services.

  Zotero and email: Zotero sign-in only gives us the user's numeric ID and
  username, never an email. That's fine, because CROSSREF_MAILTO should be your
  contact address as the app operator, set once in Vercel. Crossref uses it to
  reach whoever runs the app. Sending each user's email would also share their
  personal data with a third party.

  arXiv references:
  - arXiv IDs are now picked up from GROBID's arXiv field and from the reference
    text. That covers arXiv:…, arxiv.org/abs|pdf/…, 10.48550/arXiv.… and
    old-style IDs like hep-th/9901001. It also handles your example's URL split
    by a line break (1703. 07076).
  - They're looked up on DataCite, where arXiv registers its DOIs, and saved to
    Zotero as preprints with the arXiv ID and link.
  - If a reference has both a journal DOI and an arXiv ID, the journal DOI is
    used.
  - If DataCite has no record, the lookup falls back to a Crossref search.
  - arXiv lookups no longer count against your Crossref limit.

  Faster Crossref:
  - Polite pool: with CROSSREF_MAILTO set, Crossref allows 3 searches a second,
    3 at a time, instead of 1 and 1.
  - Pacing from Crossref's own headers: the server passes on the limits Crossref
    reports, and the browser runs lookups at that rate, in parallel up to the
    allowed number.
  - Automatic retry: a 429 no longer stops the import. The browser waits (2s,
    then 4s, then 6s), slows down, shows "The lookup service is busy; retrying
    in N s…", and continues.

  With CROSSREF_MAILTO set, a 40-reference import should run roughly three times
  faster, and arXiv references won't touch Crossref at all. Without it, you
  still get the arXiv fix and the retries, but at 1 search per second.

  To finish:
  1. Add CROSSREF_MAILTO=<your address> in Vercel (Production and Preview).
  2. Push the branch and merge the PR; Vercel deploys on merge.

  Do you want me to push it and open the PR?

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 1 file, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I read through the code for steps 1 and 3 to see how they connect. Here's how
  each step works today, what you need to do in Zotero for step 2, and where the
  hand-offs between steps are weak.

  How it works today

  Step 1: Import citations (#import)
  1. Sign in with Zotero, upload the manuscript PDF, tick the consent box, and
     click Import to Zotero.
  2. GROBID pulls out the bibliography. Each reference is looked up on Crossref,
     or on DataCite for arXiv once PR #2 is merged.
  3. Clear matches are saved to a new Zotero collection called "<filename>
     references". Uncertain ones stay on screen for you to review.
  4. Only metadata is saved: titles, authors, DOIs. No PDFs, which is why step 2
     exists.

  Step 2: Get the PDFs, in the Zotero desktop app
  1. Open the new collection, select all items, right-click, and choose Find 
     Full Text. Zotero fetches open-access PDFs, including arXiv and Unpaywall
     copies, and goes through your library's proxy if you've set one up.
  2. For paywalled papers it can't get, drag the PDF onto the item yourself.
  3. Make sure the files sync to zotero.org storage: Settings → Sync → Sync 
     attachment files in My Library using Zotero. The hosted app reads PDFs
     through Zotero's web API, which can only see files stored on Zotero's
     servers. If you sync files with WebDAV, the hosted app will never find your
     PDFs. The free tier is 300 MB.

  Step 3: Check citations (#audit)
  1. Upload the same manuscript again. This step extracts claims and references
     separately, with Anthropic, not GROBID.
  2. In Find cited sources in Zotero, pick the collection from step 1.
  3. Run the checks. For each cited source, the app looks for a matching item in
     that collection and uses it only if the item has exactly one PDF
     attachment. For arXiv papers it can fall back to the public arXiv PDF.

  Where the hand-offs break down

  Gap: The manuscript is processed twice, two different ways
  What goes wrong: Step 3 re-extracts the references with Anthropic and
    ignores step 1's GROBID results and Crossref matches. When the manuscript
    has no DOIs, the audit falls back to matching titles exactly. A subtitle
    or a slightly different title from Crossref, plus a strict author/year
    check, can leave a source marked "unavailable" even though it's sitting in
    the collection.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Gap: No way to see missing PDFs
  What goes wrong: Nothing in the app shows which items in the collection
    still need a PDF. You only find out during step 3, one claim at a time.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Gap: More than one PDF on an item
  What goes wrong: If "Find Full Text" adds a second PDF to an item that
    already has one (for example a preprint and the published version), that
    source is rejected as ambiguous.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Gap: No link between the steps
  What goes wrong: After importing, you have to go to the audit tab yourself,
    upload the file again and find the collection in the dropdown.

  Improvements I'd suggest, most useful first

  1. "Check this manuscript" button at the end of an import. It would open the
     audit with the same manuscript, the new collection already selected, and
     the matched Zotero items attached to their references. The audit would then
     find each source by Zotero item key instead of re-matching titles. This
     fixes the first gap, which is the biggest one.
  2. PDF coverage panel. After the import, and again before the audit, show "32
     of 40 have a PDF", list the missing ones, and give a "Re-check" button to
     press after running Find Full Text. It would also flag items with more than
     one PDF.
  3. Attach open-access PDFs automatically during import. For arXiv papers, the
     app could save a link to the arXiv PDF on each item, which cuts down on
     step 2. Uploading actual PDF files into Zotero through the API is possible
     but much more work, and it uses up your Zotero storage.

  Do you want me to start with the first one, the hand-off that carries matched
  items into the audit? It removes the duplicate extraction and most of the
  "unavailable" results.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The import-to-audit hand-off is built and committed on a new branch,
  import-audit-handoff (commit 9b9158d, based on main now that PR #2 is merged).
  All 149 tests pass. I haven't clicked through it in a real browser.

  The flow now

  1. Import: after references are saved, the import results show the next step:
     run Find Full Text on the collection in Zotero. Next to it is a new Check 
     citations against these sources button.
  2. Zotero: you add PDFs to that collection, as before.
  3. Click the button. The audit tab opens with the same manuscript already
     loaded and the new collection selected, and citation extraction starts on
     its own. No second upload, no hunting through the dropdown.
  4. Linking: each reference the audit finds is linked to the Zotero item you
     imported. A link is made only when there's exactly one match, on the same
     DOI, the same arXiv ID, the same bibliography text, or the title appearing
     in the reference. Linked references are labelled "linked to imported Zotero
     item", and the status line says how many were linked.
  5. Checking: for linked references, the server opens that exact Zotero item.
     It no longer needs an exact title match plus an author/year confirmation,
     which is where sources used to come back "unavailable". Results show
     "Zotero stored PDF (imported item)".

  Safety rules

  - A reference is only linked on a one-to-one match. One imported item can't
    back two references, and duplicate imports link nothing.
  - If you edit a reference's title, DOI or arXiv ID in the audit, its link is
    removed and the normal search is used instead.
  - If a linked item has been deleted from Zotero, the audit says so and falls
    back to searching the library.
  - The server only accepts well-formed 8-character Zotero item keys.
  - Changing the manuscript input or loading the example cancels the hand-off.

  Not covered yet

  - No browser test. The tests cover the button and the data it hands over, the
    linking rules, the server's use of linked items, the deleted-item fallback
    and key validation. What isn't tested is the audit tab receiving the
    hand-off in a real page. The preview deployment for the PR is the place to
    try it.
  - The audit still extracts the references again with Anthropic, because it
    needs the claims and the citation markers in the text. The difference is
    that its references now attach to your imported items instead of being
    re-matched from scratch.
  - The hand-off lives in page memory, so refreshing the page before clicking
    the button loses it. A PDF can be too large to keep in browser storage.

  Do you want me to push the branch and open a PR?

⏺ Your screenshot shows the problem better than mine would have; my headless
  screenshot hung, so I stopped it and the temporary dev server. Here's what I
  see in it, checked against the markup and CSS:

  - 51 identical cards with no information on them. Each saved reference becomes
    a full-width card reading "Reference N · Saved to Zotero", with no title,
    and each takes about 150px. That's around 7,500px of scrolling, and none of
    it needs your attention.
  - The one reference that needs you is somewhere in that list. Nothing brings
    it to the top or lets you jump to it.
  - The main actions are at the very bottom. The save button, collection link
    and next step ("Check citations") sit after all 52 cards.
  - The input form stays expanded above the results after extraction, so the
    results start well down the page. The textarea, consent text, long
    disclosure and Zotero panel all take space every time.

  Ideas, biggest gain first

  1. Compact list instead of cards. One line per reference: number, title, first
  author and year, DOI link, and a status chip (✓ Saved / ⚠ Needs review / ✗
  Not found). Only "needs review" rows open into the full editor. 52 rows would
  take about 1,800px instead of 7,500px, and each row would actually tell you
  something.

  2. Put what needs you first. Filter chips at the top, "Needs review (1) · Not
  found (0) · Saved (51) · All", defaulting to "Needs review" whenever there are
  any. Saved items collapse to one line, "✓ 51 saved to paper references:
  show", unless you open them.

  3. Sticky action bar. A bar pinned below the header showing progress
  ("Matching 23 of 52…" with a progress bar) and the one next action for the
  current stage: Stop while matching, Save 1 remaining when something needs
  review, Open in Zotero and Check citations when done. This replaces the save
  panel at the bottom and the status line that gets scrolled away.

  4. Collapse the upload area after extraction. It becomes one line: "📄
  paper.pdf · 52 references · Change file", so the results start near the top of
  the screen.

  5. A tighter upload form before extraction.
  - A drag-and-drop area for the file. Hide the paste box behind "Paste text
    instead".
  - Show Zotero sign-in as a small chip ("Signed in as amahaja5 · Sign out")
    instead of a whole column. You can't use the page without signing in anyway,
    so a full panel adds little.
  - Keep the consent checkbox right above the button. Fold the long privacy text
    into a "What gets sent where?" expander.

  6. Faster review. For a "needs review" reference, show the original reference
  and the best match side by side, with Accept and Skip buttons instead of a
  dropdown plus a checkbox. When there are several candidates, show them as a
  small list to pick from.

  Ideas 1–4 together should fit a 52-reference import on one or two screens,
  with the one reference that needs you at the top. Do you want me to build 1–4
  first, then 5–6? That work would go on the import-audit-handoff branch (not
  pushed yet), or on a separate branch if you'd rather keep the PRs apart.

         +or: 'attention', pending: 'attention', ready: 'ready', saved: '
         +saved', skipped: 'skipped' };                                  
      14 +function stateOf(row) {                                        
      15 +  if (row.saved) return 'saved';                               
      16 +  if (row.skipped) return 'skipped';                           
      17 +  if (row.selected && row.candidates[row.choice]) return 'ready
         +';                                                             
      18 +  if (row.error) return 'error';                               
      19 +  if (!row.lookedUp) return 'pending';                         
      20 +  return row.candidates.length ? 'review' : 'notfound';        
      21 +}                                                              
      22 +function counts() {

      24 +  for (const row of rows) c[GROUP[stateOf(row)]]++;            
      25 +  return c;                                                    
      26 +}

  Created PR #3 (ctrl+o to expand)
⏺ I've rebuilt the import screen around what needs your attention, and opened PR
  #3 with it: https://github.com/amahaja5/lit-graph/pull/3

  The hand-off from step 1 didn't have a PR yet, so this one PR covers both
  pieces of work. All 149 tests pass. I also rendered the page in Chrome with a
  simulated 52-reference import (50 clean matches, 1 ambiguous, 1 with no
  match), on desktop and on a phone-width screen.

  Before: 52 tall, untitled "Reference N · Saved to Zotero" cards (about 7,500px
  of scrolling), with the save button and next steps underneath them.

  After: the finished import fits on one screen:
  - Upload area collapses to one line: "graph-chemistry-preprint.pdf · 52
    references · Change manuscript".
  - Sticky bar with progress while it runs, then one next action: Stop while
    working, then Save N, then Open in Zotero and Check citations against these 
    sources, with the Find Full Text hint next to it.
  - Filter chips: Needs attention (2) · Saved (50) · All (52). It opens on what
    needs you.
  - One line per reference: a status chip (Needs review / No match / Saved…),
    the number, the title, and the first author and year.
  - Side-by-side review for the ambiguous one: your reference on the left, the
    candidate papers on the right, each with an Accept button, plus Skip. This
    replaces the old dropdown and checkbox.
  - A tighter upload form: drag-and-drop area, "Paste text instead" and "What
    gets sent where?" folded away, the consent box next to the Import button,
    and Zotero sign-in as a small account row.

  Two fixes came out of checking the screenshots:
  - While matching, the bar showed a greyed-out "Save 20" button and a
    collection-name box. They're now hidden until there's something to do.
  - On a phone-width screen, nothing runs off the right edge now.

  One problem already in main, which I didn't change: the Explore graph loads
  its charting library, D3, from cdn.jsdelivr.net. When that CDN is slow, the
  browser holds back the scripts that come after it, so the Import and
  Check-a-manuscript tabs get stuck on "Checking server setup…". I caught this
  happening in testing, not just in theory. It's described in the PR. The fix is
  small (load D3 only when the Explore tab needs it), and I can do it as a
  separate PR if you want.

  Still to try on the PR's preview deployment: import a real manuscript, run
  Find Full Text in Zotero, click Check citations, and confirm the references
  show "linked to imported Zotero item" and open their stored PDFs.

      253 +// Waits with a visible countdown; returns early if the user p
          +resses Stop.                                                  
      254 +async function countdown(ms, text) {                          
      255 +  const end = Date.now() + ms;                                
      256 +  while (!stop && Date.now() < end) {                         
      257 +    message(text(Math.ceil((end - Date.now()) / 1000)));      
      258 +    await sleep(Math.min(1000, end - Date.now()));            
      259 +  }                                                           
      260 +}                                                             
      261 +// Saves are idempotent (predetermined Zotero keys), so a rate
          +-limited step is simply repeated.                             
      262 +const MAX_SAVE_RETRIES = 5, SAVE_SPACING_MS = 300;            
      263 +async function withRetry(send) {                              
      264 +  for (let attempt = 0; ; attempt++) {                        
      265 +    try { return await send(); }                              
      266 +    catch (error) {                                           
      267 +      if (error.status !== 429 || attempt >= MAX_SAVE_RETRIES 
          +|| stop) throw error;                                         
      268 +      await countdown(Math.min(Math.max(Number(error.retryAfte
          +rSeconds) || 2, 1), 120) * 1000, s => `${error.message} Contin
          +uing automatically in ${s} s…`);                              
      269 +      if (stop) return null;                                  
      270 +    }                                                         
      271 +  }                                                           
      272 +}                                                             
      273 +async function honourBackoff(result) {                        
      274 +  if (result?.backoffSeconds) await countdown(Math.min(result.
          +backoffSeconds, 120) * 1000, s => `Zotero asked for a pause. C
          +ontinuing automatically in ${s} s…`);                         
      275 +  else await sleep(SAVE_SPACING_MS);                          
      276 +}

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've fixed it: when Zotero or Crossref says "slow down" during a save, the
  import now waits and carries on instead of stopping. The fix is pushed to PR
  #3 (commit d325dab) and all 151 tests pass.

  Why it happened: saving one reference makes about four Zotero requests (check
  the collection, check for the item, search for duplicates, create it) plus one
  lookup on Crossref or DataCite. Fifty references is roughly 250 requests back
  to back. When a service answered "too many requests", the save loop stopped.
  Lookups already retried, but saves didn't.

  What happens now:
  - Automatic retry with a countdown. For example: "Zotero is limiting requests.
    Continuing automatically in 42 s…". It waits as long as the service asks,
    and Stop still works. Retrying a save is safe: each item has a fixed key, so
    it can't create duplicates.
  - Zotero's early warnings are respected. Zotero sends a Backoff header to ask
    apps to slow down before it starts refusing requests. The server used to
    drop that signal at the end of each request. It now passes it to the
    browser, which pauses before the next save.
  - Saves are spaced slightly apart, instead of firing back to back.
  - Messages name the service: "Zotero is limiting requests" or "Crossref is
    limiting requests", not "The provider is busy".

  For the import that just stopped: everything saved before the error is still
  in Zotero. Once this is deployed, the remaining references will show as "Ready
  to save". Clicking Save N to Zotero picks up where it left off, and the items
  already saved are skipped.

  The fix will be on PR #3's preview deployment shortly, and goes to production
  when you merge.

      74 +export function semanticScholarItem(paper, paperId, doi = '') {
      75 +  const title = clean(paper?.title, 1000);                     
      76 +  if (!title) throw fail('Semantic Scholar returned no title fo
         +r this paper.');                                               
      77 +  const creators = (Array.isArray(paper.authors) ? paper.author
         +s : []).slice(0, 100).map(author => {                          
      78 +    const name = clean(author?.name, 200), parts = name.split(/
         +\s+/).filter(Boolean);                                         
      79 +    return parts.length > 1 ? { creatorType: 'author', firstNam
         +e: parts.slice(0, -1).join(' '), lastName: parts.at(-1) } : { c
         +reatorType: 'author', name };                                  
      80 +  }).filter(a => a.lastName || a.name);                        
      81 +  const types = Array.isArray(paper.publicationTypes) ? paper.p
         +ublicationTypes : [];                                          
      82 +  const journal = clean(paper.journal?.name, 300);             
      83 +  const itemType = types.includes('Conference') ? 'conferencePa
         +per' : journal ? 'journalArticle' : 'document';                
      84 +  const extra = [`Semantic Scholar: ${paperId}`, doi && itemTyp
         +e !== 'journalArticle' ? `DOI: ${doi}` : ''].filter(Boolean).jo
         +in('\n');

         +year || ''), url: `https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/${pape
         +rId}`, extra, tags: [], collections: [], relations: {} };      
      86 +  if (itemType === 'journalArticle') Object.assign(item, { publ
         +icationTitle: journal, volume: clean(paper.journal?.volume, 50)

         + } : {}) });

         +lean(paper.venue, 300);

         +clean(paper.venue, 300);                                       
      89 +  return item;

      433  ## Manuscript citation audit
      434 
      435 -Choose **Check a manuscript** in the workspace navigation. Loa
          -d an arXiv URL/identifier, upload a PDF, DOCX, TXT, or Markdow

          -ng the bibliography. The app extracts citation-bearing passage
          -s and reference mappings, lets you review/edit source titles, 
          -identifiers, passages, and citation mappings (including adding
          - missed entries), and checks the cited claims against retrieve
          -d PDFs. A passage can cite up to eight sources. Larger jobs mu
          -st be divided into sections with their bibliography (80 refere
          -nces / 150 passages per inventory).

      436 
      437  Each passage receives per-source findings and a combined asses
           sment: supported, partially supported, contradicted, or insuff
           icient evidence. Positive/negative support findings require an
            exact quoted passage anchored to a retrieved PDF page. The re
           port preserves source SHA-256, retrieval route, version, Zoter
           o keys, and reviewed PDF page indices. These are **AI-assisted
            support assessments**, not a certification of printed paginat
           ion or experimental validity. Retrieval selects relevant excer
           pts rather than assessing every page; missing evidence stays u
           nverified. Inventory extraction may miss citations and should
           be reviewed. Source PDFs without readable text need OCR outsid
           e this web workflow.

⏺ I've done everything you asked for: the Pilot labels are gone, the allowlist
  stays, there's one Zotero sign-in for the whole app, the review cart can be
  saved to Zotero, and the tabs are reordered and renamed. It's all on a new
  branch, open-zotero-signin, in two commits. All 158 tests pass, and the pages
  render in Chrome with no errors. Nothing is pushed yet.

  Allowlist: it's unchanged. I'd removed it in an unpushed commit, so I undid
  that. Only ZOTERO_ALLOWED_USERS accounts can sign in, as before.

  Pilot labels: removed from the tab name, the Import page heading and the
  README.

  Global Zotero sign-in:
  - One Sign in with Zotero button at the top right. Once you're signed in it
    shows your username and Sign out.
  - The Import and Check-citations tabs no longer have their own sign-in
    buttons, and they update when you sign in or out.
  - Sign-in always asks for write access, since importing and saving need it.
  - If someone signs in with an account that isn't on the allowlist, the header
    now says so, instead of failing silently.

  Save the review cart to Zotero (Explore tab):
  - Under the cart's export buttons there's a collection name, filled in as
    "Reading list · <seed paper title>", and a Save N to Zotero button.
  - Papers with a DOI or arXiv ID use the official Crossref or DataCite record,
    and reuse the item if it's already in your library.
  - Papers with neither (common on Semantic Scholar) get an item built from
    Semantic Scholar's details, tagged with their Semantic Scholar ID so saving
    again doesn't create a duplicate.
  - Saving again adds only papers you've added to the cart since. Renaming the
    collection starts a new one.
  - If Zotero slows you down, it waits and carries on, like the import does.
    Signed-out users are taken to sign-in first.

  Tabs: in workflow order, each named for what it does: Explore papers · Import 
  references · Check citations. The URLs haven't changed, so existing links
  still work.

  One extra fix: I changed the order the page loads its scripts, so a slow
  chart-library download can no longer leave the Import and Check-citations tabs
  stuck on "Checking server setup…".

  Not tested yet: the real Zotero sign-in popup, and saving a real cart, need a
  live Zotero account, so I couldn't check them here. The PR's preview
  deployment is the place to try both.

  Do you want me to push the branch and open a PR?

✻ Crunched for 29s · done 7:18 PM

❯ Give it a better name than lit-graph                                          

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Here are my suggestions, with a recommendation. The app now does three things:
  find papers, collect references in Zotero, and check that claims are backed
  by their sources. The name should cover all three, not just the citation graph
  that "LitGraph" describes.

  My pick: Groundwork
  - It covers both halves: you do the groundwork (finding the literature), and
    you check each claim is grounded in its sources.
  - Short, easy to say, and reads well in the header and as Zotero's name for
    the connection ("Groundwork citation import").
  - Tagline to match the three tabs: "Find the papers. Keep the references. 
    Check the claims."

  Alternatives
  - Warrant: in argumentation, the warrant is what justifies a claim from its
    evidence. It's the closest fit to citation checking and to the "justified
    citations" idea in your design doc, but it's narrower, and some people will
    read it as a legal term.
  - Sourcewise: says plainly what it does, but sounds more generic.
  - Citewise: the clearest meaning, but probably the most crowded name.

  I haven't checked whether any of these are already used by other products or
  available as domains, so do a quick search before committing to one.

  What a rename would change. The name appears in about 25 files. I'd change
  only what people see:
  - the page title, header and tagline;
  - the name shown on Zotero's authorization page and in Zotero's list of
    connected apps;
  - the sign-in messages, the User-Agent sent to Crossref, and export file
    names;
  - the README, FEATURES and the deploy docs.

  I'd leave internal identifiers alone, such as the litgraph-zotero cookie
  prefix and internal event names. Renaming the cookie would sign everyone out,
  and no user ever sees it.

  Renaming the GitHub repo and the Vercel project is separate. It changes URLs
  and the OAuth callback setup, so I wouldn't do that without you.

  Which name do you want? I'll apply it on a new branch and open a PR.

         +ce when a signed-in user loads the app, signs in to Zotero, or 
         +opens the Import panel. It does this by calling the wrapper's `
         +/health` route, which returns 503 until GROBID is ready.       
      11 
      12  The wrapper runs at 1 vCPU rather than 0.5 because Cloud Run re
          quires the first-generation environment and concurrency 1 for C
          PU below 1. With concurrency 1, a warm-up ping and an extractio
          n arriving together would start a second instance.

  Created PR #6, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The app is now Groundwork and restyled to look like Zotero. Both changes are
  in one PR: https://github.com/amahaja5/lit-graph/pull/6. All 158 tests pass,
  and the pages render in Chrome with no errors.

  Rename
  - The browser tab title and the header say Groundwork. The old tagline is
    replaced with "Find the papers. Keep the references. Check the claims."
  - Also updated: Zotero's authorization page and connected-app list, the
    sign-in messages, export file names (groundwork-review-…), the identifiers
    sent to Crossref and other services, package.json, and the docs.
  - Left alone on purpose:
    - internal litgraph-* names, because renaming the login cookie would sign
      everyone out;
    - your lit-graph.avijmahajan.com domain and the Zotero OAuth callback;
    - the Docker repository default in the Cloud Run deploy script.

  Zotero-style theme
  I took the colours from zotero.org's own stylesheet rather than guessing:
  - Zotero red #cc2936: primary buttons, the active tab's underline, and the "G"
    in the wordmark.
  - Link blue #0069e0: links, focus outlines and selected list rows.
  - Dark grey text #222 on white, light-grey #f6f6f6 panels, thin grey borders,
    small rounded corners, almost no shadows.
  - The system font (Zotero's own font, Avenir Next, needs a licence).
  - A white header with tab-style navigation. The beige backgrounds, colour
    gradients and serif headings are gone.

  It's one clearly labelled block at the end of styles.css, so it's easy to
  adjust. The graph's node colours are unchanged.

  Not covered
  - The Explore graph and review cart with real data. My screenshots used a
    stubbed API, so they show the empty graph. The PR's preview deployment is
    the place to check them with real papers loaded.
  - Your Zotero OAuth app registration (zotero.org/oauth/apps) still has the old
    name. Rename it there if you want it to match. Only you can change that.
